--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Mission Analysis:** This is an AGGREGATION mission where the score increases for every robot outside the target region (the BLACK-floored strip with BLUE walls). Wait - actually re-reading: the score increases for robots OUTSIDE the target region. The target is the BLACK strip with BLUE walls. So robots need to get to and stay in that BLACK strip.

But wait - the champion is just simple Exploration (B=0) with score ~19898. That's surprisingly good for a 1-state machine. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target is the strip with BLUE walls.

Key perceptions available:
- Floor color: BLACK (in strips) or GRAY (in middle)
- Camera: can see BLUE walls (color 3) or GREEN walls (color 2)

**Strategy:** Robots need to go toward BLUE walls. GoToColor (B=8) with clr=3 (blue) should drive robots toward the blue wall. Once there (on black floor), they should stop or stay.

The champion (simple exploration, score ~19898) beats most complex machines. This suggests robots that wander eventually end up in the black strips. But we can do better by directing them toward blue walls.

**Design approach:**
1. **Survival:** Keep champion exactly
2. **Exploitation:** Variations of simple 1-state machines and small modifications
3. **Exploration:** GoToColor blue wall machines, floor-triggered transitions

Let me design machines that go toward blue (clr=3), and when on black floor (which could be either strip - but once near blue wall it's the right strip), stay there.

Key issue: black floor exists in BOTH strips. Need to distinguish. The blue wall (clr=3) is the distinguishing feature.

**Plan:**
- State 0: GoToColor blue (B=8, clr=3) - moves toward blue wall
- When on black floor AND sees blue: stop or stay
- When on gray: go toward blue

Let me create varied machines:

FSM 1 (champion): exact copy
FSM 2-5: Small exploitations of champion (vary rwm)
FSM 6-7: GoToColor blue simple machines
FSM 8-9: 2-state machines with GoToColor blue + floor transitions
FSM 10-14: Various explorations

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key insight from Round 2:** The champion is simply `GoToColor blue (clr=3)` with score 3588. This is a massive improvement over the previous champion (19898). The strategy of going toward blue walls works extremely well.

**Analysis of top performers:**
1. Score 3588: Simple GoToColor blue (1 state) - BEST
2. Score 3997: GoAwayColor green → GoToColor blue (avoid green walls, seek blue walls) - good
3. Score 4231: GoToColor blue → Stop when on black floor - good
4. Score 4284: GoToColor blue → Exploration when on black floor - good
5. Score 4429: 3-state with GoAwayGreen + GoToBlue + Stop - good

**Gap analysis:** There's a huge gap between rank 5 (4429) and rank 6 (5844), suggesting the top 5 have a qualitatively better structure. Another huge gap between rank 8 (8880) and rank 9 (17808).

**Strategy for Round 3:**
- **Survival:** Keep champion (GoToColor blue, 1-state)
- **Exploitation:** Fine-tune the top 4-5 machines with small parameter changes
  - The GoToColor blue + Stop on black floor variant (rank 3) could be improved
  - The GoAwayGreen → GoToBlue variant (rank 2) is promising
  - Adjust probabilities slightly
- **Exploration:** Try variants with:
  - GoToBlue + FlockCenter (once in the strip, aggregate)
  - GoToBlue + Stop (when sees blue clearly)
  - 3-state machines with GoAwayGreen, GoToBlue, Stop
  - Different stop conditions (neighbor count, seeing blue)

The key behavior: robots should go to blue wall. Once there (on black floor near blue wall), they could stop. The 2-state machine (GoToBlue → Stop when black) is rank 3 at 4231 - slightly worse than pure GoToBlue. So stopping may not help much. 

Let me focus on variations of the top performers:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key insight from Round 3:** The champion is a 2-state machine:
- State 0: GoAwayColor green (clr=2) → transition to state 1 when sees blue (clr=3, p=0.9)
- State 1: GoToColor blue (clr=3) → transition to state 0 when on black floor (p=0.85)

This is elegant: robots flee green walls, and when they spot blue walls they go toward them. When they arrive (black floor), they briefly go back to fleeing green (which keeps them pressed against the blue wall area).

**Score gap analysis:**
- Rank 1: 2971 (champion)
- Rank 2-3: ~3561 (simple GoToBlue)
- Rank 4-5: ~3972-4106 (GoAwayGreen + GoToBlue variants)
- Rank 6-8: ~4350-4547
- Big gap after rank 8 (4547 → 5318)

**Exploitation strategy:**
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Vary the champion's parameters: p values (0.9→0.85, 0.85→0.8 or 0.9), try adding a second transition
3. Try swapping state order (GoToBlue first, then GoAwayGreen)
4. Try adjusting the transition probability on seeing blue
5. Add a third state (Stop when on black + sees blue)

**Key parameters to vary:**
- p0x0 (probability of transitioning when sees blue): try 0.8, 0.85, 0.95, 1.0
- p1x0 (probability of transitioning when on black): try 0.7, 0.8, 0.9, 0.95
- Add more transitions to each state

**Exploration:**
- 3-state: GoAwayGreen → GoToBlue → Stop (when on black + sees blue)
- Try FlockCenter as a holding behavior in the blue strip
- Try neighbor-count conditions to keep robots clustered

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 4:**

The top machines are all very similar 2-state GoAwayGreen→GoToBlue machines. The scores are tightly clustered (2873-3394 for top 10), with a big gap to rank 13 (13345).

**Key observations:**
- Champion: p0x0=0.85 (sees blue → switch), p1x0=0.9 (on black → switch back)
- Rank 2: p0x0=0.9, p1x0=0.85 (slightly different)
- Ranks 3-5: similar, very close scores
- The 2-state GoAwayGreen/GoToBlue topology is clearly dominant
- Adding extra transitions (rank 12: score 3967) hurts slightly
- 3-state with Stop (rank 8: 3309) is decent
- Reversed order (rank 9: GoToBlue→GoAwayGreen) also decent (3337)

**Key parameter space to explore:**
- p0x0 (transition from GoAwayGreen when sees blue): {0.7, 0.75, 0.8, 0.85, 0.9, 0.95, 1.0}
- p1x0 (transition from GoToBlue when on black): {0.7, 0.75, 0.8, 0.85, 0.9, 0.95}
- Best so far: (0.85, 0.9) → 2873; (0.9, 0.85) → 2930

**Strategy:**
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Fine-tune parameters around the champion
3. Try p0x0∈{0.8, 0.82} and p1x0∈{0.88, 0.92, 0.95}
4. Try adding a gray floor condition to help transition
5. Try the GoToBlue first variant with adjusted params
6. Explore 3-state variations that improve on rank 8

The scores are very close together (2873-3008 for top 5), suggesting we're near a local optimum. Need to explore both fine-tuning AND genuinely different approaches.

For exploration: try using `--c1x0 1` (gray floor) instead of `--c0x0 0` (black floor) as the transition condition - gray floor means robot is in the middle, so GoAwayGreen should kick in. Actually the current logic makes sense: on black floor (in a strip), switch from GoToBlue to GoAwayGreen.

Let me try varying both probabilities systematically and also explore some new ideas:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 5:**

The scores are extremely tightly clustered (2944-3487 for top 13), with all good machines using the same GoAwayGreen→GoToBlue 2-state topology. The champion (2873) wasn't beaten this round, though several came close.

**Key parameter observations:**
- p0x0 (sees blue → switch to GoToBlue): best values seem to be 0.80-0.88 range
- p1x0 (on black → switch to GoAwayGreen): best values seem to be 0.88-0.92 range
- The 2-state topology with minimal transitions is consistently best
- Adding extra transitions generally hurts
- The "reversed" order (GoToBlue→GoAwayGreen) is slightly worse (~3344)

**Gap analysis:**
- Scores 2944-3069 for ranks 1-9: very tight
- Gap to rank 10 (3200): small
- Gap to rank 12 (3344): moderate  
- Gap to rank 13 (3488): moderate
- Gap to rank 14 (4295): bigger

We seem to be converging to a narrow region. The champion has (p0x0=0.85, p1x0=0.9). Let me try finer-grained search around this area and also some genuine explorations.

**Fine-tuning grid around champion:**
- p0x0 ∈ {0.78, 0.80, 0.83, 0.85, 0.87}
- p1x0 ∈ {0.88, 0.90, 0.92, 0.94}

**Explorations:**
- Try using gray floor (c=1) condition instead of black floor in state 1 - on gray means robot is in the middle, so it should go toward blue
- Try adding a very small random transition to unstick robots
- Try 3-state with different structure

The key insight: in state 1 (GoToBlue), the condition `on black floor, p=0.9` causes the robot to switch back to GoAwayGreen when it reaches the target. This creates a dynamic where robots oscillate at the blue wall boundary. Maybe a slightly lower probability to stay longer in GoToBlue once arrived?

Actually wait - `--c1x0 0` is BLACK floor, p=0.9. When on black (arrived at a strip), it switches to GoAwayGreen. But both strips have black floor! So sometimes robots might switch in the wrong strip. Using `--c0x0 7 --l0x0 3` (sees blue) is more discriminating. The transition FROM GoAwayGreen when it SEES blue is the smart one.

For the return transition (black floor in GoToBlue), maybe we want a lower probability so robots stay longer in GoToBlue when they arrive. Let me try p1x0 values of 0.7, 0.75.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 6:**

The champion (2873) still holds. This round's best was 2931 (p0x0=0.78, p1x0=0.9). Scores are extremely tight (2931-3046 for top 10), with a big gap to rank 11 (3864).

**Key findings:**
- Best p0x0 values: 0.78, 0.80, 0.83 (lower seems slightly better)
- Best p1x0 values: 0.9, 0.88 (0.9 consistently good)
- The 2-state, 1-transition-per-state topology is clearly optimal
- Adding ANY extra transitions hurts (ranks 11-12 at 3864-3941)
- 3-state machines fail badly (rank 13: 12743)
- The topology is locked in - just need to find the exact right probabilities

**Parameter landscape:**
- p0x0 (sees-blue threshold in GoAwayGreen state): champion=0.85, but 0.78 and 0.80 are performing well this round
- p1x0 (on-black threshold in GoToBlue state): champion=0.9, consistently best

The scores have high variance (same machine scored 2981 vs 2988 across rounds), so we're sampling noise. The true optimum is in the range p0x0∈[0.75, 0.90], p1x0∈[0.85, 0.95].

**Strategy:**
1. Keep champion exactly (2873)
2. Fine-tune p0x0 in range [0.70, 0.85] with focus on lower values
3. Fine-tune p1x0 around 0.90
4. Try a few genuinely different explorations to escape local optima

**Explorations to try:**
- Very low p0x0 (0.65, 0.70) - maybe even less frequent switching helps
- Different l values: what if we use a different blue color code? No - clr=3 is blue, that's correct
- Try using `--c0x0 0` (black floor) condition in state 0 instead of seeing blue - might help robots in wrong strip
- Try `--c1x0 7 --l1x0 3` (sees blue) in state 1 to stop when at blue wall

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 7:**

The champion (2873) still holds from Round 4. This round's best was 2882 (p0x0=0.85, p1x0=0.9) - essentially the same machine as the champion, just a different random seed giving a slightly different score.

**Key observations:**
- The scores are extremely noisy - the "same" machine (p0x0=0.85, p1x0=0.9) scored 2882 this round vs 2873 (champion) and 2981/2989 in earlier rounds
- The true performance difference between machines in range p0x0∈[0.65, 0.87] is very small
- p1x0=0.9 consistently appears in top performers
- p0x0=0.85 is the champion's value, and it ranks 1st again this round
- Lower p0x0 values (0.65-0.80) are competitive but not clearly better

**What we know:**
- Topology is fixed: GoAwayGreen → (sees blue) → GoToBlue → (on black) → GoAwayGreen
- Best p0x0: ~0.85 (champion) with 0.77-0.83 also competitive
- Best p1x0: ~0.90 (consistently)
- Any extra transitions hurt significantly

**Strategy for Round 8:**
Given we're at round 8/10 with only a few rounds left, I should:
1. Keep the champion exactly
2. Focus on fine-grained parameter search around (0.85, 0.9) - maybe try very small deviations
3. Try a few bolder explorations that might reveal a qualitatively better machine
4. The scores are noisy enough that re-running similar machines might catch a lucky low score

**Fine-tuning:** Try p0x0 ∈ {0.84, 0.85, 0.86, 0.87} × p1x0 ∈ {0.89, 0.90, 0.91}

**Bold explorations:**
- What if both states use GoToBlue (no GoAwayGreen)? Probably worse.
- What if we use `--c0x0 0` (black floor) as condition in state 0 too? Robots switch to GoToBlue when on black - could help escape wrong strip.
- What about using neighbor count conditions?
- Try the exact champion topology but with color 1 (red) instead of 3 (blue) - no, that makes no sense.
- Try GoAwayGreen with higher rep gain

Actually, a key insight I haven't tried: what if the transition condition from GoToBlue is NOT "on black floor" but rather "sees blue" (already sees the target) or "sees green" (went to wrong strip)? Let me try a variant where state 1 transitions when it SEES GREEN (meaning it went to the wrong strip).

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 8:**

New champion: 2864.05 with a new variant! The key change: in state 1 (GoToBlue), the transition condition is now `--c1x0 7 --p1x0 0.9 --l1x0 2` - meaning it transitions back when it **SEES GREEN (color 2)** rather than when on black floor. This is semantically better: if a robot is heading toward blue and suddenly sees green, it means it went to the wrong side or is near the green wall - time to turn around.

**Key insight:** The champion uses:
- State 0 (GoAwayGreen): transition when sees BLUE (l=3, p=0.85) → go toward blue
- State 1 (GoToBlue): transition when sees GREEN (l=2, p=0.9) → go away from green

This is more discriminating than "on black floor" - it avoids false triggers in the wrong strip.

**What to explore:**
1. Vary p values around (0.85, 0.9) for the sees-green condition
2. Try different l values in state 1 (what color triggers return to GoAwayGreen?)
3. The "sees green" condition (l=2) in state 1 is the breakthrough - exploit this

**Parameter grid:**
- p0x0 (sees blue → GoToBlue): {0.80, 0.83, 0.85, 0.87, 0.90}
- p1x0 (sees green → GoAwayGreen): {0.85, 0.88, 0.90, 0.92, 0.95}

**Explorations:**
- Try l1x0=3 (sees BLUE in GoToBlue → stay? No, transition back to GoAwayGreen when sees blue again doesn't make sense)
- What about using "sees green" in BOTH transitions?
- Try lower p0x0 values (0.75, 0.78)
- Keep some "on black floor" variants as they were also competitive

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 9:**

New champion: 2793.98 with p0x0=0.90, p1x0=0.9, l1x0=2 (sees green → go away from green). Very close second: 2794.81 with p0x0=0.82, p1x0=0.88.

**Critical observation:** Ranks 7-11 all scored EXACTLY 2853.90 with p0x0=0.85 but varying p1x0. This identical score suggests they hit a deterministic floor or the same random seeds - the simulator is giving the same result for these variants. This means at this parameter level, p1x0 variation doesn't matter much when p0x0=0.85.

**Best parameters found:**
- Champion: p0x0=0.90, p1x0=0.90 → 2793.98
- Rank 2: p0x0=0.82, p1x0=0.88 → 2794.81
- Rank 3: p0x0=0.87, p1x0=0.90 → 2808.95
- Rank 4: p0x0=0.83, p1x0=0.90 → 2814.02

The pattern suggests p0x0 around 0.87-0.92 with p1x0≈0.90 is the sweet spot. The "sees green" condition (l=2) in state 1 is confirmed as better than "on black floor".

**This is the final round (10/10)** - need to maximize chances of beating the champion.

**Strategy:**
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Dense sampling around p0x0∈{0.88, 0.89, 0.90, 0.91, 0.92, 0.93, 0.95} × p1x0∈{0.88, 0.90, 0.92}
3. Try p0x0=1.0 (always transition when sees blue)
4. A few wild explorations: try different l values, try GoToBlue first

The scores are noisy (~50-100 variation), so running multiple close variants gives good coverage.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
